You have trapped me with my own words... Hmph! Why should I speak with you and needlessly bring agitation upon my mind? Say whatever comes into your mouth. Call the *Ramayana* a storehouse of obscenity; shout in condemnation that the *Bharata* is utterly vile! What do I care!! Anger will arise only if I attach value to your words...

You will feel no anger at all? Truly?

From now on I shall simply think, “What is it to us? Let the madwoman babble whatever nonsense she pleases,” and leave it at that...

Whatever I say?

Yes! Yes! Yes!

Good heavens! Have you become that bold? You will feel no anger? Hmph! Even if I say Rama and Sita were brother and sister, will you not grow angry?

You are the one saying it. With your extraordinary intelligence, go ahead and say Rama and Sita were brother and sister... Whom does Sita marry in that high-handed *Ramayana* of yours...?

Leave aside whether it is my *Ramayana* or a high-handed *Ramayana*. I too say that Sita marries Rama.

It was Rama whom she married—but Sita...

Sita was Rama’s sister...

Ponni! Why do you speak so outrageously, maligning the Lord...?

### Image 10

What anger have I towards Rama or Sita? Ask whether what I say is wrong or right and learn the truth yourselves... Why do you glare at me as though you would burn me to ashes? Rama and Sita are brother and sister! It is that brother and sister who marry! That is the Rama play found in Java!

Ponni! Are you telling the truth? Is the *Ramayana* like this in Java?

Yes! Why, did your husband not tell you that...?

No! He said that even on the island of Java our *Ramayana* continued to be honoured.

In that Javanese *Ramayana*, Rama and Sita are brother and sister. I shall send you the book written by a traveller to Java. Read it yourself...

Enough, dear! I feel my head spinning. Some quarrelsome rogue has written down all manner of things as he pleased. Who would believe any of this?

That is exactly what I say. If various people in ancient times wrote all manner of things according to their whims, can we stubbornly insist that all of it remains right even now? My principle is that we cannot.

I do not know, dear. The elders repeatedly told us that Rama was Mahavishnu and Sita was Lakshmi Devi herself. Crores upon crores of people like me hold that belief—that is all! Do not say all manner of things and destroy the faith of people like us.

Is that alone what the “elders” have said? One person has even said that Sita is the soil, and Rama is the grass—or the crop!

Who was that man, dear? What blessed soul called Rama the crop and Sita, who is Mahalakshmi, the soil...?

### Image 11

Now you are asking properly. I merely repeated what Rabindranath Tagore—who won the regard of that blessed soul Mahatma Gandhi and earned the name of a great poet—said.

Tagore said that...?

Would I lie? Tagore has indeed written so. Even four days ago I saw that book in our Pattu’s house...

Does Pattu too read books of this kind?

I do not know whether she reads them or not. I saw the book lying upon Pattu’s table...

After I have read and examined it, I shall speak with you again...

Until then, I shall not open my mouth on that subject. I myself will bring you the book...

No, no. I know the way—I shall go... What is this, dear? There is a great din in the street... What are they shouting must be abolished...? These days we hear only cries that this must be abolished and that must be abolished.

Yes! Whatever people now recognise as harmful can be ended only if they themselves oppose and abolish it. They say the discus weapon came in the Puranic age to destroy evil. Will it come like that now...?

It will not come, Ponni! It certainly will not! Would the Lord send his discus weapon into a world in which all of you exist...?

Why should he not send it...? Was it not he who sent us here too...?

One can only say he committed that act by mistake...

The Lord made a mistake? Alas! Even you are speaking sinful words. Ought you to speak so...?

### Image 12

I am not going to speak any more, Ponni. I am going to give you a resounding slap on the cheek...

That is how the devotees apparently slapped one another during the Vadakalai–Tenkalai quarrel. A policeman named Kannusami came from Kanchipuram to the neighbouring house. He said the police were in desperate difficulty by the time they separated the Vadakalais and Tenkalais and stopped the fight... Do you know why? Some among the police themselves bore the Tenkalai mark, and some the Vadakalai mark!!! Are you suppressing your laughter...? Hmph! Is laughter laughter only when one laughs aloud...?

Ponni! Deeds of this kind are wrong and must be condemned. Everyone is equal before the Lord. Quarrelling over whether one bears the Tenkalai or Vadakalai mark is madness...

You may say so. Ask them, and their devotion will make them declare, “We are all prepared to give even our lives for our exalted right!” Is it not out of devotion that they study the Vedas and recite the *Tiruvaymoli*...?

Yes... Wait! Wait! Ponni! What is this? The noise is growing louder and louder... I hear cries of “Alas! Alas!”... Even a police whistle...

Yes... What is this...? Is there some Vadakalai–Tenkalai quarrel here too...?

Fie! Come, let us go and see...

Why should we trouble ourselves...? Here comes Pattu. She must have seen everything directly before coming...

I saw it, I saw it. Even I could make no sense of it...

Pattu saw it but could not understand...? What a marvel...

Pattu! What happened...? What is that commotion...?

## Part 9

### Image 1

What am I to say? Viewed from one side, I must say that a *bhajan* procession took place and that, at certain people’s instigation, the police beat and tormented its members, wounding the devotees’ minds and injuring their bodies. Arguing from the other side, I must say that a mob of hooligans shouted frenziedly and insulted respectable people; when the “great men,” pained by this, tried to stop them, the mob defied them and resorted to violence, whereupon the police beat and dispersed the hooligans and restored peace...

Enough, enough, enough! It is enough that you studied to be a lawyer, and enough that you torment the life out of us. When we ask what the commotion in the street is, can you not give a brief, clear answer? You instantly turn into a lawyer... Tell us what happened.

What happened...? Some people are proceeding through the street singing *bhajans*, and the police are beating and chasing them... The blows fell not only upon the *bhajan* party, but upon those who went to watch as well...

Pattu! What is this—blind tyranny? Did you hear, Ponni? The police are apparently beating people who are proceeding with *bhajans*... What an outrage...

Some people ask that too... But all the prominent men are saying that what the police did was just!

Pattu! Explain it clearly...

Generally—that is, without viewing the two sides as plaintiff and defendant... Is that what you mean...?

Yes... yes... Tell us what happened without considering who might pay you...

Very well! First I shall say what I saw. The police are chasing and beating the *bhajan* party. Instead of fleeing in terror, the party takes the blows and conducts its *bhajan* in between them. This commotion began near the shopping street!

### Image 2

What concern is it of the police if people sing *bhajans*?

There is no direct enmity between the police and the *bhajan* party. The police came only because the wealthy merchants of the shopping street complained and asked them to beat and disperse the party and protect the merchants.

Enough of riddles, Pattu. Tell us the details in a way we can understand...

That is what happened. But do you know what the *bhajan* party was singing in the shopping street...?

Some litany of divine names known to them! “Jai! Jai! Vitthala!” perhaps, or “Kanda! Kadamba!” What does it matter to these men which one it was?

You may ask what it matters to them, and I may agree. But could the wealthy merchants in the shopping street remain indifferent no matter what was sung?

Did you hear the justice she speaks of, Ponni? People are walking along the street worshipping the Lord in whatever manner each prefers... What is wrong in that? Tell me, Ponni!

Can it be stated so generally...? If one stood before Murugan’s sanctum and sang, “Govinda, you will dissolve my invincible evil karma,” would those who had come to worship Murugan remain quiet?

Ponni has made a very important “point.” Now, the law does not prevent each person from eating according to his preference. But would it be right for someone lodging in a vegetarian’s house to fry dried fish...?

This is a matter concerning God, Pattu! Do not go all the way to dried fish!

Whether a case concerns God or dried fish, law is law... Why, Pattu! Is that not so...?

### Image 3

A sound argument! I mentioned a lodger frying dried fish in a vegetarian’s house, and I was not speaking merely for the sake of it. A case about this came before the High Court—a sensational case. It was a suit to evict the tenant. One side argued that so long as the rent was paid regularly, the tenant need not vacate and the law would not permit eviction. The other argued that the law entitled a landlord to evict one whose manner of eating violated vegetarianism and wounded a vegetarian’s feelings.

Tell us the result and stop your lawyer’s performance, Pattu dear!

The result? After the matter went to the Court of Appeal, judgement was given for the landlord! The person who cooked and ate dried fish had to leave the house.

Pattu! Who delivered the judgement in the Court of Appeal? Was he a Sastri, a Sarma or an Iyengar...?

Sampurnananda Dikshitar! Why do you ask his name, Ponni? Dikshitar is a tiger in the law, dear...

Goodness, lawyer! Enough of the tiger pouncing over dried fish. Now tell us about the shopping-street affair.

I did not mention this without a reason. If one or several people deliberately perform any act intended to wound one or several other people, everyone must surely agree that the law may prevent it...

Yes... What I am now discussing is whether the police may beat and disperse people who sing *bhajans*. Is there such a law? Do not forget that and talk about something else. At this rate, I do not know what rubbish you will produce after becoming a lawyer.

There is no law saying that people who sing *bhajans* may be beaten and dispersed...

### Image 4

Even when political meetings are prohibited, they grant permission for *bhajans* to be conducted.

Ponni makes a sound “point”! The law does not interfere in religious matters—as far as possible...

But now the law tells the police to beat those who conduct *bhajans*... because our people are running the state... That is why...

There is nothing of the kind. Although what occurred was called a *bhajan*, it was one only in name; in truth it was no *bhajan* at all... It was a frenzied act that used the name of *bhajan* to humiliate the wealthy merchants of the shopping street...

How so? What is this, Pattu? Your talk is a new kind of riddle.

When the law is taken up and explained to ordinary people, it will seem like a riddle. Had what those people conducted truly been a *bhajan*, the wealthy merchants of the shopping street would not have become so furious. They would not have sent for the police. Under the name of *bhajan*, certain people deliberately attempted to demean and disgrace the trade and business conducted by the wealthy merchants in the shopping street...

Is that so...? How?

While claiming to conduct a *bhajan*, they could have sung sacred songs or *kavadi chindus* about the Lord—“Lord of Pandharpur,” “Beloved of Parvati,” “Twelve-handed one”—could they not?

Yes. That is what a *bhajan* means. Even those who cannot sing can simply say, “Hare Rama! Hare Krishna!”

Yes... That is a *bhajan*... But did these people conduct one? Do you know what they sang while parading through the shopping street? “Do not run a black market! God will not tolerate it! Do not sell at unjust prices! The Lord will...”

### Image 5

“...not approve of it!” Such a song! With drums, rhythm and melody!

That does not amount to a *bhajan*, Pattu! But singing that there must be no black market and that God will not approve of it seems like good instruction, does it not...?

Pattu! Instead of each person singing *bhajans* to the Lord to seek a good fate for himself, is it not more praiseworthy to conduct *bhajans* with songs like these in order to seek the welfare of the community?

One could argue so too... One might argue it and win the case. That is a separate matter. But does singing like this in the shopping street not humiliate the people there...?

How can you declare that so absolutely?

These people sing, and upon hearing them, many others look at the merchants, laugh and jeer at them. Is that a song? Is that a *bhajan*? “If you hide what you have, lightning will strike—lightning will strike your head! What is in the shop! The stock that is there! If you hide what you have, lightning will strike!” There are witnesses who say that when they sang this, people there pointed their hands and said, “The lightning will strike that shopkeeper’s head!”

Pattu! Leave the matter of witnesses aside. Who conducted this *bhajan*?

I inquired. Apparently it was some group that runs a Podhumarai Forum...

Good heavens! That is the forum run by my younger brother. Is that whom the police are beating?

Ponni! Was it your brother who conducted the *bhajan*? We must not let this go. We must file a case. How can the police beat people who sang with the noble aim of seeking the community’s welfare...?

Pattu! We can see to that later... I must go and see my brother.

### Image 6

Come! Come! Let us all go.

Not merely go—we shall sing as we go, and go on singing as we proceed...

10

Ponni, why are you all smiles? Is it not your habit always to look as though you were immersed in weighty thought? They speak of “a festival unlike any other day”; in just that way, your face is so radiant today. What is the matter, dear? Come, tell me.

“There would be something to tell only if anything were new! My face is just as it always is. What new thing have you discovered in it...?”

“If you ask that way, how can I answer? I do not know, dear. If it is some great secret, do not tell me; keep it within your own heart.”

“There is no secret at all. Why imagine things needlessly?”

“I do not believe you in the least.”

“Leave that aside—has the lawyer not come here?”

“No... What is this, Ponni! The moment she is mentioned, your face turns into a firework...”

“Your saying ‘like a firework’ is fitting in one way. Do you know why? The moment a firework is lit, it gives a beautiful light... Once it goes out, it turns entirely black...”

“Do I need you to tell me that before I can know it...?”

### Image 7

“I did not say it so that you might know! Say instead that I said it to show that I know.”

“Was the firework’s glitter while it burns and its turning to charcoal when it goes out something you needed to tell me now?”

“The moment you said my face looked like a firework, the firework came to my mind. Go on with you.”

“Ponni! You are becoming more and more of a rogue... Enough speaking in circles. Tell me the truth. What made your face look, as they say, like a red lotus the moment Pattu was mentioned?”

“Do you not know? That day, when some men came to beat my younger brother in the shopping street, did we not all run there...?”

“Yes, we ran there and raised a clamour, and the disturbance became a dead snake... What of it...?”

“Did you not properly observe Pattu, who came running with us...? Do you remember how shaken she became, and how she insisted that your brother’s wounds must be treated at once?”

“Yes! As though I could forget. Pattu was indeed frantic.”

“Tell me why she became frantic.”

“What sort of question is this, dear? Would she not be alarmed if someone dear to us met with danger or hardship?”

“She would be alarmed... But she would not melt away! Our Pattu here simply melted the moment she saw my younger brother... Do you understand the matter?”

“Is that so...? I did not notice, Ponni!”

### Image 8

“My younger brother too is a fine fellow. He usually looks like the very cat of whom the proverb asks, ‘Would this cat drink milk?’ But do you know what became of him that day? When he saw Pattu melting, he began to melt too.”

“Ponni! If so...”

“Pattu will come to our house very soon...”

“Splendid! Splendid! Has the story taken such a turn? But, Ponni, the people in Pattu’s house follow the old almanac. Will they consent?”

“They will not consent. Does one need divination to know that? But is Pattu a woman ignorant of the facts? She has studied law.”

“Is everything settled merely because she studied law? Can a bottle-gourd drawn in a book flavour a curry...?”

“My younger brother will ask Pattu that very question...”

“He will ask Pattu?”

“Yes. At this very moment, the two of them must be discussing this matter.”

“Is Pattu at your house? Did she not see you leaving for here?”

“Why would she not? She saw me! I asked, ‘Shall I come too?’ She said, ‘You go and return; I have a little work.’”

“After saying that, she remains at your house? Just look at her courage! When did she arrive there?”

“She is there every day. Is she not a lawyer...? Must she not take a statement from my brother?”

“So Pattu goes there! That is why she has not been seen on this side at all.”

“There was no blow at all, only a hidden wound! There is nothing to fear, my brother said. Pattu...”

### Image 1

“Pattu immediately said, ‘It may not be a mere hidden bruise; there could be an internal injury. It must be attended to at once, or it may become very dangerous.’”

“I did not notice, Ponni! Was Pattu talking with him?”

“Must she speak to him directly? Was I not there—the postwoman! The message passed through me! She spoke to me, but only if her gaze had been in my direction...”

“Go away. Perhaps you have needlessly misunderstood her. Pattu was speaking to you, was she not...?”

“She is a lawyer! Yet that day she became a doctor! What does that mean? Let it pass. Let us suppose that only on that day her heart somehow could not bear it and she grew frantic. What business brought her the next day? She arrived at the house carrying seven or eight law books.”

“What were the law books for?”

“My brother asked the same thing. She began flinging heaps of authorities before him and insisting, ‘If you do not wish to respect my words, disregard them; that does not matter. But look at the law books yourself. A case must be filed against the big men of the shopping street...’”

“She is a lawyer, dear! She desires to conduct a case and win fame for her skill. If a case is filed over their attempt to beat your brother in the shopping street, she can conduct it herself and earn a good name. Pattu must have had that thought. That is why she came! You have imagined it differently. That is all there is to the matter.”

“My husband said just the same! But am I not the one who knows Pattu’s purpose?”

“Ponni! Listen to this! The elders have said that what the eye sees is false and what the ear hears is false. I feel that your idea about Pattu is the same kind of thing.”

### Image 2

“Do not speak loudly. Keep what you feel within your heart. I hear footsteps... Pay attention...”

“Yes... Ponni! It is she. Pattu is coming, dear.”

“Do not let her know even the least bit about the matter. Draw her out and see—she will pour it all out by herself.”

“Pattu! Come! Come! Are you conducting some great murder case...? You are never seen on this side.”

“What is that—do you think conducting a murder case is a great feat...? Murder cases have become utterly commonplace. If a lawyer is to display her ability, she must obtain a case that demands a new interpretation and a distinctive meaning for the law.”

“What sort of case could arise that permits one to argue by giving the law itself a new explanation...?”

“Why not! Do you know that clouds are pursued, struck and cooled to make artificial rain fall...?”

“Ponni, when I am asking Pattu about a case, why do you interrupt like a great judge...?”

“Great judges do not ramble on this way!”

“Give it to her like that, Pattu dear! Only then will Ponni’s mouth be subdued.”

“But it is best to speak with an open heart. Yes. That is what I like. Look at this younger brother of hers—good heavens, the only thing left was for him to have been born mute! We may speak for half an hour and ask a thousand and eight questions, but he simply will not open his mouth.”

“Pattu! Because many people do not understand that manner of his, some even condemn him as arrogant...”

### Image 3

“But do you know why he does not open his mouth and speak much? He does not know how to speak maturely, affectionately and intelligibly. My younger brother is a simpleton, Pattu!”

“Is endlessly speaking about everything one sees the conduct of an intelligent person? Can one say so? Your brother is neither a simpleton nor conceited... I somehow think he feels a peculiar dislike whenever he sees me...”

“Why does Pattu say that, dear? Ponni, why should your brother dislike Pattu? What is the reason?”

“I do not think it is dislike. He is extremely shy about speaking with new people...”

“This is fine, dear! Women alone are supposed to be shy! Why should he be shy? Though Pattu had scarcely known him before, she goes and speaks to him. What hardship is there in his answering her? Shyness? Why, Pattu, did you torment him with questions he could not answer?”

“Nothing of the kind. For four days I have gone and asked him: ‘It would be good to conduct the case. It will create a stir throughout the town. The malpractices of those who reap plunderous profit will be exposed. Your association’s influence in the town will grow. So consent to filing the case; I shall take care of everything else.’ But only if he would even say ‘Hmph’...”

“He simply lies there grinning. That is his habit.”

“He neither grins nor grimaces. As I speak and speak, your brother keeps a gentle smile upon his face.”

“Leave him aside. After learning that this is how he is, why do you go back and speak with him again and again, Pattu...?”

“Repeated blows will move even a grinding stone. Is my younger brother more hopeless than a grinding stone...?”

### Image 4

“Today he gave half his consent...”

“Did you see? Pattu is a clever woman. She has returned only after obtaining his consent.”

“Yes... What great peculiarity is there in that case? It is only a case of assault, is it not? A riot and disturbance, is it not...?”

“As Pattu says, it will not be an ordinary assault case...”

“Just wait until the case is tried—you yourselves will be astonished! New subtleties of law will emerge one after another.”

“Yes, I stopped midway through the case concerning artificial rain. Pursuing clouds for artificial rain is an offence in law!”

“What offence is there in it?”

“What offence could there be? Who could call it an offence and bring a case?”

“A case could be brought. Suppose the government of one country engages in encircling and cooling a bank of clouds so that rain will fall upon its own country. The people of the country towards which that bank of clouds had been travelling could bring suit! They could say: ‘The bank of clouds was coming towards my country. It would have rained upon my country! The land would have prospered! The harvest would have been abundant! By blocking and halting the bank of clouds that was coming towards us and causing rain to fall upon its own country, that government committed an offence equivalent to highway robbery of my country’s property.’ That country could bring such a suit...”

“Splendid! Splendid! What Ponni says is apt. That case contains a complicated legal question...”

“Watching the two of you leap with excitement merely makes me laugh. Would anyone file a suit claiming that clouds had been intercepted and taken away...?”

“Why should they not? Do you know that last month a case concerning a book was tried in a manner that astonished the whole world?”

“Did someone enter a bookshop and steal the book?”

“No! A famous writer wrote a book. The government banned it, saying that the matters described in it were obscene, repulsive and capable of corrupting the moral sense. The publishing house that issued the book filed suit, contending that the government’s ban was invalid...”

“What book was it, Ponni, that brought matters to the point of a lawsuit?”

“It is only a story! But it tells of a nobleman’s wife who, unable to obtain conjugal pleasure from her husband, formed a secret illicit relationship with the gardener of the estate.”

“To think one would feel moved to write such a story! Why, Ponni! Must one write precisely what is obscene? Should what ought to be hidden not remain hidden? Would people with even a little refinement read such a book? If you asked me, I would say it was not enough merely to ban the book; the fellow who wrote it ought to have been forbidden from writing anything ever again...”

“That is what you would say, because only your own likes and dislikes matter to you. Is that not what you would say? But, Pattu, you ought to know—the case concerned the book *Lady Chatterley’s Lover*...”

“Oh! Of course I know. I read the entire account of the case in the newspaper...”

“Was this case reported in our newspapers? I did not notice it at all!”

### Image 6

“You would not have noticed. Many people are like that. You would have looked only at the Coimbatore counterfeit-currency case.”

“Yes, yes! I turn to another page only after reading that case’s entire proceedings.”

“Do you know what happened in this book case? The judge gave the book to the jurors and told them, ‘Read it and see whether it contains anything obscene, repulsive or capable of corrupting morality, as alleged.’ They went into an inner room and read the whole thing.”

“This is more amusing than everything else, Ponni! They gave them the very book that had been banned to read!! All right! After reading it...?”

“The jurors said that it did not appear to contain obscenity warranting a ban. The ban was lifted.”

“Not in our country. They have said it may not be sold here. It is not banned in other countries.”

“In other countries there may be no ban not only upon the book, but even upon the exploits of ladies like the one in it. We want no such storehouse of obscenity in our country, Ponni dear!”

“Yes, yes! Our country has no need of a storehouse of obscenity such as *Lady Chatterley’s Lovers*. Is not the *Bharata* alone enough for our country...?”

“They will pull out your tongue and cut it off, Ponni, if you speak like this outside... The *Bharata* may be a storehouse of obscenity to you, because you are a great genius! To crores upon crores of people it is a sacred story, Ponni—a sacred story!”

“Good heavens! Let it be. Who said it should not? You say that the story of Lady Chatterley must contain no obscene matter—certainly! The *Bharata* is a story connected with God...

“Good heavens! Is this what you are making out to be a great crime? A man once ran breathlessly to the police. ‘What? The police listened to him and made extensive preparations, only to discover that a man was cutting down a bunch of bananas in the garden! He had confused *kolai*, ‘murder,’ with *kulai*, ‘bunch,’ and babbled; the police were deceived by what they heard...”

“Not bad! It would be good if the police were that energetic. In Kanchipuram, bold thefts have apparently continued for four months...

## Part 11

### Image 1

11

“Mani! Do you understand it properly?”

“Do not crave empty prestige and end up harming Pattu’s health, dear. If need be, take her yourself and show her to some senior doctor to learn why the faintness occurred and how it may be prevented. Do not mix up and give her something, saying, ‘I am a doctor; would I not know?’”

“Your thinking that I would have no concern for Pattu is itself wrong. You must also understand doctors properly. Doctors must never consider—and will not consider—their own hollow prestige. If the nature of an illness is not clearly understood, there is neither disgrace nor fault in taking the patient to a senior doctor.”

“Yes, Manonmani! Some people say, ‘What medicine could anyone else possess that I do not? If I cannot understand it, who else could possibly understand it?’ and...”

“Such people are unfit even to remain in the sacred field of medicine. If they are thought to have entered this profession from greed for money, great harm will befall society itself. After completing medical school, we begin this sacred service only after taking an oath.”

“Do not be angry that I said it, Mani! Very well! What caused the faintness?”

“At times the nerves may become slack, or excessively tense. This faintness arises from that.”

“The elders call it the mischief of the wind...”

### Image 2

“It is fortunate Ponni is not here. If she were...?”

“If she were, she would confound us by asking, ‘Who saw the ghost?’ But, Manonmani! Do you know how keen Ponni’s intelligence is...? Many people... leave many people aside—creatures like me believe whatever anyone says. But Ponni will never accept anything without investigating it...”

“Had people failed to investigate, no progress would have occurred in the world. Take our medical profession itself. There was a time when people blindly believed that illness arose from God. They thought disease would disappear through conducting worship, kneeling before a priest, a knotted thread, or water over which an incantation had been recited. This was not only here! Even in Western countries that displayed wonders of every kind! Fear of and belief in magicians, witches and goblins persisted for a very long time even abroad. People believed witches caused diarrhoea, fever, convulsions and paralysis. If they saw some poor, dishevelled old woman, they would stone her, imprison her, tie her up and burn her...”

“They call the West a place inhabited by people who know everything... Even there, did such things happen...?”

“Yes! What is astonishing in that? Before clarity of knowledge arose, it was like our country there too...”

“Manonmani, our country’s traditional belief is that people fall ill and suffer according to the nature of each one’s ‘past karma.’”

“Not only in our country, but in every country. When the causes of disease and the medicines for them were unknown, people in every country believed illness was a severe punishment imposed by God...

### Image 3

“Now it has been explained that diseases arise from germs—insects invisible even to the eye, which can be seen only through a magnifying instrument. Medicine exists to destroy those creatures! And medicine exists to prevent them from gathering again!

“Pattu has only a nervous disorder. A nervous disorder may come from a loss of strength; it may arise from excessive labour beyond one’s capacity; it may come from sudden joy, grief or shock. Suppose you are lying down and half asleep. Something falls upon you—cold! What becomes of you then...?”

“The heart pounds violently, and the body trembles all over...”

“Some people’s bodies turn cold.”

“Sweat pours out...”

“That is what happens. Fear causes it...”

“Do you see—fear itself becomes a kind of illness. In the same way, if the body or mind is shocked for any reason, illness arises. Whether it becomes severe or not depends upon the strength in the body. Pattu possesses the necessary degree of strength. So the faintness will not grow into an illness or recur often and trouble her. The moment I gave her an injection, Pattu felt relief.”

“You gave her an injection...? Now you are talking... That is why the illness went away immediately...”

“You yourself say that now. Do you know how strongly she resisted the injection at first...?”

“Even now I do not like having injections, Mani! It is not right for doctors to give an injection for everything either...”

### Image 4

“I too think that an injection should be given only on certain occasions. Some people nowadays have even developed a fondness for having an injection for everything. A lady regularly comes to me for treatment...”

“Why did you say ‘a lady’ without giving her name...? As though I would not know her name.”

“I did not give the name precisely because you would not know her. She belongs to a landed proprietor’s household. Whenever that lady comes to see me, she says something or other: ‘I have had no sleep; my arms and legs ache all over; my head is pounding; my stomach hurts; I have no appetite.’ Then she says, ‘Give me some injection...’”

“Does it not hurt her?”

“Why should an injection hurt? Before piercing the skin, we apply medicine to that spot so that no pain is felt...”

“Yes, yes! If that medicine were not applied, would those who take injections not writhe in pain...?”

“Is it only for giving injections? When major operations are performed, the present method is to inject medicine so that the place where surgery is required and the area around it become numb...”

“Manonmani! The moment you said ‘operation,’ something came to mind. They remove a baby from its mother’s womb by performing an operation. That is a wonder, dear—a great wonder.”

“That has become utterly commonplace now! Do you know what operations are performed today? If there is pain in the heart, a method has arisen of operating even there, examining it, applying medicine and setting it right.”

“The heart is the most important thing of all; they call it the vital channel...

### Image 5

“Yes. Even if some nerve in it has become damaged, they can cut it open, examine it and put it right. Such extraordinary progress has occurred. If one had a splitting headache, what would we have done before...?”

“My grandmother would mix cobweb with lime and apply it. The splitting headache would disappear in an instant.”

“That was in those days! If one had chest pain, they ground stag’s horn and applied it; if one had stomach pain, they smeared on sandal paste! It is not like that now...”

“At some monastery... I do not remember the name... they apparently prepare and give an oil for a splitting headache. If one rubs it in and bathes the head for forty days, the headache disappears entirely, they say.”

“There are splitting headaches that no oil or medicinal ash will cure. Excessive heat makes the head split; an unending cold makes it split; abundant worry and agitation cause a splitting headache. Medicine will cure all these. But some people develop a sore inside the head, or unwanted flesh grows there.”

“Flesh grows...? Inside the head?”

“Yes, yes! Weeds sprout of themselves beside a crop, do they not? Only when the weeds are removed will the crop grow luxuriantly... But what would happen if we set about weeding? Instead of pulling out the weed, would we not destroy the crop itself? In the same way, a lump of flesh that the body does not need may sprout...”

“Inside the head...?”

“Yes! If it were on the outside, it would be visible and could be treated easily...”

“How can anyone know that a lump has sprouted inside the head?”

### Image 6

“There is a way. If a doubt arises, a needle is inserted into the backbone and a little of the fluid running there is taken and tested. That reveals whether any lump of flesh has sprouted inside the head...”

“Hmph! Go on, go on. The more I hear, the more wondrous it is...”

“Once the presence of a lump is known, it must be cut out... Otherwise it will grow and grow, damaging and destroying the other things within the head...”

“How can something inside the head be cut out?”

“They do remove it! They lift away as much of the skull as necessary and cut out the lump of flesh visible within.”

“In what foreign land does this wonder occur, dear?”

“It has now occurred even in our country.”

“Very well! Say that after a little while they will treat us as they do a motorcar: if a part is damaged, they will remove it and fit another...”

“Must even a little while pass? They are doing all that already! If an eye is lost, they fit another eye.”

“Do you mean what they call a glass eye?”

“Nonsense! That is an antiquated method! I mean one like our own eye. If one person’s eye is damaged, they remove it, take another person’s good eye, place it there and stitch it in.”

“Why, this sounds like the story of Kannappa Nayanar...”

“That is a story! This is something happening now!”

### Image 7

“When blood poured from the Lord’s eye, Kannappa Nayanar is said to have plucked out his own eye and made it the Lord’s eye...”

“A story! A sacred story illustrating the glory of what a devotee can do for God! But this is an age in which humans themselves give eyes to other humans.”

“Do they say, ‘Take out my eye’?”

“Yes! How long is an eye useful to us? As long as we are alive! Afterwards?”

“Afterwards! We become a handful of ashes.”

“And the eye? It goes to the earth! Is that not so? Instead, after death, one can give the eye to a human being! Before dying, many people put it in writing that their eye may be taken for someone else. The moment they die, the sound eye is removed and carefully preserved. As I said, it is that eye which is used for people who have lost their sight...”

“Noble souls who give even after death, Mani! Such people! Until now I had heard only of donating blood...”

“Blood donation was one cause of Pattu’s faintness.”

“What is this, dear! Did Pattu donate blood?”

“No! No! She saw someone donating blood... That alone made her faint!”

“Why did she go there?”

“Because she had already donated her heart to the man who donated his blood...”

“Enough of your medical language, dear! Explain it intelligibly.”

“Ponni’s younger brother is keenly interested in public service. So he agreed to donate blood. Apparently he had donated blood once before as well. He went to donate it again. Our Pattu here has lost her heart to him! She accompanied him and tried to stop him, saying, ‘Please do not donate blood; your health will be ruined.’ He did not listen and donated it. The moment she saw it, she fainted. She recovered herself. Afterwards, whenever she saw him, she kept speaking of that alone! So she fainted again!”

### Image 8

“Is that the matter! Was this the cause of the faintness...?”

“Poets sing that there could be no cause more worthy of faintness than this...”

“Of course they sing! What does it cost them? They make songs out of the suffering borne by someone else! Why would they not sing? If it befell them, they would know what that faintness is... Leave that aside. Why, Manonmani dear, Pattu will not faint again, will she...?”

“There she comes herself. Ask her.”

“Very well... And Ponni is with her! My, my—it looks as though no one can prevent it now! Come, you modern women! Where are you coming from—the flower garden or the bookshop...?”

“What is this! Today your welcome contains mockery from the very moment we arrive. Mani! What? Did you come ahead and wrap up the whole story...?”

“She neither tied a knot nor gave me a clue. I know everything myself. Pattu’s elder uncle is very hot-tempered. Ask him first, obtain his consent, and then let the marriage arrangements proceed. Do not act rashly, do something disastrous, and become caught in trouble and conflict...”

## Part 12

### Image 1

“Pattu! Listen! When I tell you something, you grow angry and angry!”

“Pattu! Ponni’s younger brother is a golden man. He is a suitable match for you. His caste is different, to be sure—but I too agree that in this age it ought not to be treated as important. But there is your elder uncle. You know him yourself; his anger flares in an instant...”

“Even he apparently dislikes caste and its divisions! Pattu says so.”

“Is he alone like that? Why, Ponni! Would I tell you something you do not know? In this age no one speaks—or will speak—to say that caste distinctions must continue. But when it comes to action, you know how many people turn somersaults and change sides...”

“Tell Pattu. I know that this is how many people behave.”

“If she goes and asks her elder uncle, is he going to consent? He will pull a long face and say: ‘Your wish! Why ask me? Who am I? Who are you? Your mother and father were fortunate! They went to God without witnessing your sinful deed!’”

“He will slap her cheek! He will abuse her indiscriminately! He will say, ‘You will not receive a quarter-anna!’ He will say, ‘Your foot must not enter my house!’ He will threaten, ‘Alas, that man is dangerous! I shall see to the fellow who comes to marry you!’”

“What is this? It sounds as though something disastrous may happen...”

“We can inform the police of the situation beforehand and obtain protection... under the law...”

“Leave the law aside, Pattu dear! Do not keep speaking like a lawyer without understanding the time and occasion...

### Image 2

“Ponni! What if we did this? Let four of us go and explain the matter to him...”

“I cannot be one of those four! They will say I am speaking for my younger brother.”

“That too is true. Shall I do one thing? My husband often says something about Pattu’s elder uncle... Apparently he lives for a feast! Shall I invite him to our house for a feast one day, give him a meal that makes him happy, and then gently begin speaking of the marriage?”

“Will your husband agree to this?”

“Why do you worry about that, dear? That is my affair. Tell me whether the plan will succeed if we give him a feast and then speak.”

“What do we know? We have no such experience.”

“Ponni! I can give your mocking words a proper lash. Let it pass. Since arrangements for a good deed are under way, I shall ignore it. Leave that aside. Why, doctor dear? You spoke of all manner of wonders being performed. Tell me, is there any medicine to calm a hot-tempered man? We shall give it to Pattu’s elder uncle.”

“Splendid! Splendid! You began with a feast and ended with medicine. I am not saying this in jest! A hot temper, sudden sadness and causeless elation all depend upon the condition of the nerves. Researchers now say that a person’s entire character and conduct depend upon the structure and nature of the nerves.”

“Are you saying, Mani, that if the nature of the nerves is changed, even the character will change?”

“Yes! They say extraordinary successes will be achieved in this field within a few years.”

### Image 3

“If so, say that there will be no trouble once the disorder in Pattu’s elder uncle’s nerves is set right.”

“That could be done. Or Pattu’s nervous disorder could be removed, and even the love that has flared in her heart could be erased so completely that no trace of it remains.”

“If you are going to keep talking this way, I am going out...”

“Look at the anger—look at the anger! We shall speak just so. Who are you to stop us? Wherever you go, we will not leave you alone. We shall come there too and speak of this very thing. What can you do about it...?”

“Are you asking what I can do? Are you asking me?”

“Yes, madam lawyer, yes! What will you do?”

“Do not... Do not provoke me...”

“We are not afraid of this threat...”

“That is what you say now. You will understand when the summons arrives...”

“Summons...? For whom...? For us...?”

“I shall bring a suit stating: ‘They deliberately mocked me over and over and shocked my mind. That shock caused illness. I was unable to practise law for three months. I consequently suffered a loss of three thousand rupees.’ What do you people know of the law...?”

“Go away! You and your law...”

“Do you imagine, then, that I said it as a joke? A suit can be brought under the law... If someone disturbs your peace of mind, subjects you to shock, or frightens you—the law permits suits for all these things. Do you know...?”

### Image 4

“My, my! In a few days her madness will grow so extreme that she will probably sue Ponni’s younger brother himself. A woman who says she can sue us for disturbing her peace of mind might even bring a suit claiming that he stole her very heart, dear...”

“Nyeh, nyeh, nyeh! Your barbed words have neither meaning nor relevance...”

“Gone! Your case is gone, Pattu dear! If you file suit saying, ‘These people’s mockery shocked my mind,’ and then yourself declare that their words had neither meaning nor relevance, your side will lose...”

“Ponni speaks law, Pattu. But in her present condition, she cannot even explain to herself who the plaintiff is and who the defendant is!”

“Poor thing.”

* * *

## Translator's Notes

- `ரங்கோன் கமலம்` is retained as “Rangoon kamalam,” a period jewellery term used in contrast with a genuine diamond; no more specific modern stone or material is silently inferred.
- The dialogue deliberately distinguishes `துறவி` (*thuravi*, “renunciant”) from `தவசி` (*tavasi*, “ascetic”); the transliterated forms are retained where the characters’ wordplay depends upon that distinction.
- `POONA — Peoples' Organisation Orbit New Asia` is reproduced as the comic expansion given by the character, not presented as the historical name of an organisation.
- The dialogue’s contemporary medical and scientific claims are translated as printed period speech and are not silently updated to modern terminology or medical knowledge.
- The identifiable book referred to as `சாட்டர்லி சீமாட்டியின் லீலைகள்` is rendered by its published English title, *Lady Chatterley’s Lover*.
